Like I’ll be talking about the delivery trucks, then somehow it’s Thursday, then the church grill we used last summer, then my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me. I can see it on their faces, and then I lose what I was trying to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed your mood feeling unusually high, euphoric, or irritable, along with having much more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, unusually high, like happy-high? Not really. I get irritated sometimes, sure, but that can be because someone changes the display plan and then the whole aisle looks wrong, and then you’re thinking about the shipment tags, and the tags never stick right anyway.\n\nEnergy… I wouldn’t say more than usual. I still feel like me, mostly. I’m not bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard, seen, or sensed things that other people around you didn't seem to notice—like voices, sounds, or visions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing someone standing there who isn’t there or anything. Sometimes I hear a noise and then I’m trying to figure out if it was the back door at work or the cart wheels, because those carts have that one bad wheel that wobbles and it sounds like—well, it throws me off.\n\nBut voices, or visions… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like a history of head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, or heavy alcohol or drug use—that started around the same time as these speech and thinking difficulties?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t connected it to anything medical like that. I didn’t have some big event where I hit my head and then suddenly couldn’t talk right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSeizures… I don’t think so. Thyroid, I don’t know much about that. Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I haven’t really noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like a history of head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, or heavy alcohol or drug use—that started around the same time as these speech and thinking difficulties?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t connected it to anything medical like that. I didn’t have some big event where I hit my head and then suddenly couldn’t talk right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSeizures… I don’t think so. Thyroid, I don’t know much about that. Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this pattern of your thoughts and speech jumping around like this—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I guess… months, not just days. I don’t think so… not special abilities or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I wonder if people at work are looking at me funny, but that’s probably because I’m taking too long to explain something or I’ve gone off about the stockroom counts when they asked about the register.

Plotting, no… I don’t know. “Watching” is a tricky word because in retail everybody watches everybody a little, you know? Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’m not bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard, seen, or sensed things that other people around you didn't seem to notice—like voices, sounds, or visions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing someone standing there who isn’t there or anything. Sometimes I hear a noise and then I’m trying to figure out if it was the back door at work or the cart wheels, because those carts have that one bad wheel that wobbles and it sounds like—well, it throws me off.\n\nBut voices, or visions… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like a history of head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, or heavy alcohol or drug use—that started around the same time as these speech and thinking difficulties?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t connected it to anything medical like that. I didn’t have some big event where I hit my head and then suddenly couldn’t talk right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSeizures… I don’t think so. Thyroid, I don’t know much about that. Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this pattern of your thoughts and speech jumping around like this—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I guess… months, not just days. Maybe around six months now? That’s when people started noticing it more on the sales floor, like I’d be explaining something and then I’d sort of jump to the wrong part, or stop because I was thinking about the display or the tags or whatever.\n\nIt wasn’t like one morning it started, but… yeah, about six months it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any strong beliefs lately that others might find unusual or hard to believe—like feeling that people are watching you, plotting against you, or that you have special abilities—even if you're not sure they're true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special abilities or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I wonder if people at work are looking at me funny, but that’s probably because I’m taking too long to explain something or I’ve gone off about the stockroom counts when they asked about the register.\n\nPlotting, no… I don’t know. “Watching” is a tricky word because in retail everybody watches everybody a little, you know? Cameras, managers, customers waiting for help, and then the district person comes in and suddenly everyone stands up straighter. But I don’t have some strong belief like that, I don’t think. Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting. Like with paperwork, if I’m doing the closing sheets, I’ll start looking at one number and then think about whether the display table was set right, and then I’m not on the sheet anymore.\n\nReading… I don’t read as much as I used to, but that could just be because after work I’m tired, or there’s the TV on, or the mail is piled up and then I remember I was supposed to move the laundry—so it’s hard to say.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting important things exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?

It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Not that I can say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have others noticed you doing things that seemed unusual or out of place for the situation—like odd behaviors at work, trouble keeping up with personal care or hygiene, or acting in ways that didn't fit what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. I don’t know if they’d say “unusual,” but at work I’ll realize I’ve been walking the same aisle for a while, like back and forth, fixing the same row of batteries. Those little packs are always crooked anyway, because people take one from the middle instead of the front, and then the hooks don’t line up—\n\nBut, yeah, sometimes I’m not really moving on to the next thing. Or I’ll be standing there holding the schedule clipboard, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go, and I’m just… looking at it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one they put on the busy weekend shifts, you know? Saturdays were always a mess—people with coupons that expired, returns without receipts, the whole line backed up—and I could usually keep it moving.

Now they don’t really trust me to run those shifts anymore. My boss said after my performance review that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. He said it kind of carefully, like he didn’t want to make it sound too harsh, but that’s what it was.

And at home… my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle. Commitments with people, plans, that kind of thing. Sometimes people seem uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she smooths it over. So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.

Cameras, managers, customers waiting for help, and then the district person comes in and suddenly everyone stands up straighter. But I don’t have some strong belief like that, I don’t think. Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting. Like with paperwork, if I’m doing the closing sheets, I’ll start looking at one number and then think about whether the display table was set right, and then I’m not on the sheet anymore.\n\nReading… I don’t read as much as I used to, but that could just be because after work I’m tired, or there’s the TV on, or the mail is piled up and then I remember I was supposed to move the laundry—so it’s hard to say.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting important things exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Not that I can say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have others noticed you doing things that seemed unusual or out of place for the situation—like odd behaviors at work, trouble keeping up with personal care or hygiene, or acting in ways that didn't fit what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. I don’t know if they’d say “unusual,” but at work I’ll realize I’ve been walking the same aisle for a while, like back and forth, fixing the same row of batteries. Those little packs are always crooked anyway, because people take one from the middle instead of the front, and then the hooks don’t line up—\n\nBut, yeah, sometimes I’m not really moving on to the next thing. Or I’ll be standing there holding the schedule clipboard, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go, and I’m just… looking at it. Then I have to sort of snap back and say, okay, registers or stockroom or whatever. So I guess that probably looks odd.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were doing at work and with relationships before these speech and thinking difficulties started, would you say your overall performance and functioning have noticeably declined since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one they put on the busy weekend shifts, you know? Saturdays were always a mess—people with coupons that expired, returns without receipts, the whole line backed up—and I could usually keep it moving.\n\nNow they don’t really trust me to run those shifts anymore. My boss said after my performance review that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. He said it kind of carefully, like he didn’t want to make it sound too harsh, but that’s what it was.\n\nAnd at home… my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle. Commitments with people, plans, that kind of thing. Sometimes people seem uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she smooths it over. So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like I’ll be talking about the delivery trucks, then somehow it’s Thursday, then the church grill we used last summer, then my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me. I can see it on their faces, and then I lose what I was trying to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed your mood feeling unusually high, euphoric, or irritable, along with having much more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, unusually high, like happy-high? Not really. I get irritated sometimes, sure, but that can be because someone changes the display plan and then the whole aisle looks wrong, and then you’re thinking about the shipment tags, and the tags never stick right anyway.\n\nEnergy… I wouldn’t say more than usual. I still feel like me, mostly. I’m not bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard, seen, or sensed things that other people around you didn't seem to notice—like voices, sounds, or visions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing someone standing there who isn’t there or anything. Sometimes I hear a noise and then I’m trying to figure out if it was the back door at work or the cart wheels, because those carts have that one bad wheel that wobbles and it sounds like—well, it throws me off.\n\nBut voices, or visions… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like a history of head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, or heavy alcohol or drug use—that started around the same time as these speech and thinking difficulties?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t connected it to anything medical like that. I didn’t have some big event where I hit my head and then suddenly couldn’t talk right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSeizures… I don’t think so. Thyroid, I don’t know much about that. Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this pattern of your thoughts and speech jumping around like this—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I guess… months, not just days. Maybe around six months now? That’s when people started noticing it more on the sales floor, like I’d be explaining something and then I’d sort of jump to the wrong part, or stop because I was thinking about the display or the tags or whatever.\n\nIt wasn’t like one morning it started, but… yeah, about six months it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any strong beliefs lately that others might find unusual or hard to believe—like feeling that people are watching you, plotting against you, or that you have special abilities—even if you're not sure they're true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special abilities or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I wonder if people at work are looking at me funny, but that’s probably because I’m taking too long to explain something or I’ve gone off about the stockroom counts when they asked about the register.\n\nPlotting, no… I don’t know. “Watching” is a tricky word because in retail everybody watches everybody a little, you know? Cameras, managers, customers waiting for help, and then the district person comes in and suddenly everyone stands up straighter. But I don’t have some strong belief like that, I don’t think. Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting. Like with paperwork, if I’m doing the closing sheets, I’ll start looking at one number and then think about whether the display table was set right, and then I’m not on the sheet anymore.\n\nReading… I don’t read as much as I used to, but that could just be because after work I’m tired, or there’s the TV on, or the mail is piled up and then I remember I was supposed to move the laundry—so it’s hard to say.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting important things exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Not that I can say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have others noticed you doing things that seemed unusual or out of place for the situation—like odd behaviors at work, trouble keeping up with personal care or hygiene, or acting in ways that didn't fit what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. I don’t know if they’d say “unusual,” but at work I’ll realize I’ve been walking the same aisle for a while, like back and forth, fixing the same row of batteries. Those little packs are always crooked anyway, because people take one from the middle instead of the front, and then the hooks don’t line up—\n\nBut, yeah, sometimes I’m not really moving on to the next thing. Or I’ll be standing there holding the schedule clipboard, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go, and I’m just… looking at it. Then I have to sort of snap back and say, okay, registers or stockroom or whatever. So I guess that probably looks odd.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were doing at work and with relationships before these speech and thinking difficulties started, would you say your overall performance and functioning have noticeably declined since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one they put on the busy weekend shifts, you know? Saturdays were always a mess—people with coupons that expired, returns without receipts, the whole line backed up—and I could usually keep it moving.\n\nNow they don’t really trust me to run those shifts anymore. My boss said after my performance review that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. He said it kind of carefully, like he didn’t want to make it sound too harsh, but that’s what it was.\n\nAnd at home… my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle. Commitments with people, plans, that kind of thing. Sometimes people seem uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she smooths it over. So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, has this disorganized thinking and speech been present most of the time on an ongoing basis, or has it come and gone in separate episodes with periods where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: It’s been more… ongoing, I think. Not like I have a bad week and then I’m totally back to myself for a month or something.\n\nThe jumbled talking, or when I’m trying to explain something and it gets all tangled up—that’s been noticeable for about six months now. Same with the odd stops on the sales floor, where I’m just sort of stuck there and then realize people are waiting. Some days are better than others, sure, especially if the store’s quiet, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely normal in between.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure I’d call it motivation, exactly. I mean, I still think about doing things. Like at home I’ll see something that needs doing and I’ll think, okay, I should get to that, but then—well, sometimes it’s more that I get caught up in how to start it.

Hobbies… I don’t know. I used to tinker with little things around the house, but then you need the right screwdriver, and the little ones disappear somehow. I had a whole set in a drawer, and then one of them was in the junk bowl by the keys, which doesn’t make sense because that’s where the grocery cards are supposed to go.

So… maybe? I haven’t really noticed it as just not wanting to do things. It’s hard to tell.

So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, has this disorganized thinking and speech been present most of the time on an ongoing basis, or has it come and gone in separate episodes with periods where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: It’s been more… ongoing, I think. Not like I have a bad week and then I’m totally back to myself for a month or something.\n\nThe jumbled talking, or when I’m trying to explain something and it gets all tangled up—that’s been noticeable for about six months now. Same with the odd stops on the sales floor, where I’m just sort of stuck there and then realize people are waiting. Some days are better than others, sure, especially if the store’s quiet, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely normal in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your motivation to start or finish things you used to want to do—at home or with hobbies—even when you had the time and energy to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it motivation, exactly. It’s not like my legs are locked, exactly. I don’t think it’s frozen like that. It’s more that I get… caught, I guess.

Like I’ll be on the sales floor and realize I’ve been pacing the same aisle, or fixing the same row of batteries again and again, even though they’re already straight. Or I’ll be holding the schedule clipboard and the associates are waiting for me to say who goes where, and I’m just standing there with it.

I could probably move if someone snapped me out of it. It’s just in the moment I don’t seem to… shift to the next thing. The batteries are right by the register endcap, and they’re always a mess because people grab the wrong size—AA, AAA, those little coin ones—and then I’m there longer than I should be. So, no, not frozen exactly. More stuck in the action or in my head.

Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this pattern of your thoughts and speech jumping around like this—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I guess… months, not just days. Maybe around six months now? That’s when people started noticing it more on the sales floor, like I’d be explaining something and then I’d sort of jump to the wrong part, or stop because I was thinking about the display or the tags or whatever.\n\nIt wasn’t like one morning it started, but… yeah, about six months it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any strong beliefs lately that others might find unusual or hard to believe—like feeling that people are watching you, plotting against you, or that you have special abilities—even if you're not sure they're true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special abilities or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I wonder if people at work are looking at me funny, but that’s probably because I’m taking too long to explain something or I’ve gone off about the stockroom counts when they asked about the register.\n\nPlotting, no… I don’t know. “Watching” is a tricky word because in retail everybody watches everybody a little, you know? Cameras, managers, customers waiting for help, and then the district person comes in and suddenly everyone stands up straighter. But I don’t have some strong belief like that, I don’t think. Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting. Like with paperwork, if I’m doing the closing sheets, I’ll start looking at one number and then think about whether the display table was set right, and then I’m not on the sheet anymore.\n\nReading… I don’t read as much as I used to, but that could just be because after work I’m tired, or there’s the TV on, or the mail is piled up and then I remember I was supposed to move the laundry—so it’s hard to say.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting important things exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Not that I can say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have others noticed you doing things that seemed unusual or out of place for the situation—like odd behaviors at work, trouble keeping up with personal care or hygiene, or acting in ways that didn't fit what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. I don’t know if they’d say “unusual,” but at work I’ll realize I’ve been walking the same aisle for a while, like back and forth, fixing the same row of batteries. Those little packs are always crooked anyway, because people take one from the middle instead of the front, and then the hooks don’t line up—\n\nBut, yeah, sometimes I’m not really moving on to the next thing. Or I’ll be standing there holding the schedule clipboard, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go, and I’m just… looking at it. Then I have to sort of snap back and say, okay, registers or stockroom or whatever. So I guess that probably looks odd.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were doing at work and with relationships before these speech and thinking difficulties started, would you say your overall performance and functioning have noticeably declined since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one they put on the busy weekend shifts, you know? Saturdays were always a mess—people with coupons that expired, returns without receipts, the whole line backed up—and I could usually keep it moving.\n\nNow they don’t really trust me to run those shifts anymore. My boss said after my performance review that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. He said it kind of carefully, like he didn’t want to make it sound too harsh, but that’s what it was.\n\nAnd at home… my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle. Commitments with people, plans, that kind of thing. Sometimes people seem uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she smooths it over. So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, has this disorganized thinking and speech been present most of the time on an ongoing basis, or has it come and gone in separate episodes with periods where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: It’s been more… ongoing, I think. Not like I have a bad week and then I’m totally back to myself for a month or something.\n\nThe jumbled talking, or when I’m trying to explain something and it gets all tangled up—that’s been noticeable for about six months now. Same with the odd stops on the sales floor, where I’m just sort of stuck there and then realize people are waiting. Some days are better than others, sure, especially if the store’s quiet, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely normal in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your motivation to start or finish things you used to want to do—at home or with hobbies—even when you had the time and energy to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it motivation, exactly. I mean, I still think about doing things. Like at home I’ll see something that needs doing and I’ll think, okay, I should get to that, but then—well, sometimes it’s more that I get caught up in how to start it.\n\nHobbies… I don’t know. I used to tinker with little things around the house, but then you need the right screwdriver, and the little ones disappear somehow. I had a whole set in a drawer, and then one of them was in the junk bowl by the keys, which doesn’t make sense because that’s where the grocery cards are supposed to go.\n\nSo… maybe? I haven’t really noticed it as just not wanting to do things. It’s hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself 'stuck' staring at the clipboard or standing in the aisle, does your body actually feel frozen or unable to move, or are you just lost in your thoughts while your body could move if you tried?\n\nPatient: It’s not like my legs are locked, exactly. I don’t think it’s frozen like that. It’s more that I get… caught, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be on the sales floor and realize I’ve been pacing the same aisle, or fixing the same row of batteries again and again, even though they’re already straight. Or I’ll be holding the schedule clipboard and the associates are waiting for me to say who goes where, and I’m just standing there with it.\n\nI could probably move if someone snapped me out of it. It’s just in the moment I don’t seem to… shift to the next thing. The batteries are right by the register endcap, and they’re always a mess because people grab the wrong size—AA, AAA, those little coin ones—and then I’m there longer than I should be. So, no, not frozen exactly. More stuck in the action or in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========

I haven’t really noticed it as just not wanting to do things. It’s hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself 'stuck' staring at the clipboard or standing in the aisle, does your body actually feel frozen or unable to move, or are you just lost in your thoughts while your body could move if you tried?\n\nPatient: It’s not like my legs are locked, exactly. I don’t think it’s frozen like that. It’s more that I get… caught, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be on the sales floor and realize I’ve been pacing the same aisle, or fixing the same row of batteries again and again, even though they’re already straight. Or I’ll be holding the schedule clipboard and the associates are waiting for me to say who goes where, and I’m just standing there with it.\n\nI could probably move if someone snapped me out of it. It’s just in the moment I don’t seem to… shift to the next thing. The batteries are right by the register endcap, and they’re always a mess because people grab the wrong size—AA, AAA, those little coin ones—and then I’m there longer than I should be. So, no, not frozen exactly. More stuck in the action or in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave people close to you—like your wife or coworkers—commented that your facial expressions or tone of voice seem less emotional or more flat than they used to be, even when you're talking about something that would normally make you happy or upset?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if anyone’s said it that way, like “flat.” My wife says I mumble sometimes, or that I sound like I’m not listening, but that’s not exactly the same thing, is it?

At work people mostly just want to know where they’re assigned, you know, register two or stocking or returns, and if I’m too quiet they’ll ask again. But I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks different. I mean, faces are funny anyway—some people smile at customers all day and then the minute they turn around it’s gone. Retail does that to you.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed comments about my expressions or my voice being less emotional. Not specifically.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F29\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in. I'm Dr. [Name]. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because at work, when I’m trying to explain things—like inventory numbers, or who’s on the weekend schedule—it starts out okay and then my words kind of… jump around. Like I’ll be talking about the delivery trucks, then somehow it’s Thursday, then the church grill we used last summer, then my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me. I can see it on their faces, and then I lose what I was trying to say in the first place.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you noticed your mood feeling unusually high, euphoric, or irritable, along with having much more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, unusually high, like happy-high? Not really. I get irritated sometimes, sure, but that can be because someone changes the display plan and then the whole aisle looks wrong, and then you’re thinking about the shipment tags, and the tags never stick right anyway.\n\nEnergy… I wouldn’t say more than usual. I still feel like me, mostly. I’m not bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard, seen, or sensed things that other people around you didn't seem to notice—like voices, sounds, or visions?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like seeing someone standing there who isn’t there or anything. Sometimes I hear a noise and then I’m trying to figure out if it was the back door at work or the cart wheels, because those carts have that one bad wheel that wobbles and it sounds like—well, it throws me off.\n\nBut voices, or visions… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any medical conditions—like a history of head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, or heavy alcohol or drug use—that started around the same time as these speech and thinking difficulties?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t connected it to anything medical like that. I didn’t have some big event where I hit my head and then suddenly couldn’t talk right, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSeizures… I don’t think so. Thyroid, I don’t know much about that. Alcohol or drugs, no—nothing heavy like that. It’s more like it just started being noticeable when people at work would ask me something simple and then I’d get pulled off into the side details, like the schedule board or the delivery slips, and then nobody’s following me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this pattern of your thoughts and speech jumping around like this—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: I guess… months, not just days. Maybe around six months now? That’s when people started noticing it more on the sales floor, like I’d be explaining something and then I’d sort of jump to the wrong part, or stop because I was thinking about the display or the tags or whatever.\n\nIt wasn’t like one morning it started, but… yeah, about six months it’s been noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any strong beliefs lately that others might find unusual or hard to believe—like feeling that people are watching you, plotting against you, or that you have special abilities—even if you're not sure they're true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not special abilities or anything like that. I mean, sometimes I wonder if people at work are looking at me funny, but that’s probably because I’m taking too long to explain something or I’ve gone off about the stockroom counts when they asked about the register.\n\nPlotting, no… I don’t know. “Watching” is a tricky word because in retail everybody watches everybody a little, you know? Cameras, managers, customers waiting for help, and then the district person comes in and suddenly everyone stands up straighter. But I don’t have some strong belief like that, I don’t think. Nothing I’d say I’m sure of.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your speech getting derailed, have you noticed other changes in your thinking—like forgetting things you'd normally remember, losing track of tasks, or having trouble concentrating on things like reading or paperwork?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting. Like with paperwork, if I’m doing the closing sheets, I’ll start looking at one number and then think about whether the display table was set right, and then I’m not on the sheet anymore.\n\nReading… I don’t read as much as I used to, but that could just be because after work I’m tired, or there’s the TV on, or the mail is piled up and then I remember I was supposed to move the laundry—so it’s hard to say.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m forgetting important things exactly. It’s more like I get pulled away from what I’m doing, but I don’t know if that’s new-new or if people are just noticing it more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how you feel or interact with others lately—like feeling less interested in things you used to enjoy, wanting to be alone more, or feeling emotionally flat or distant from people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still talk to people at work because I have to, that’s the job, right? Customers come in and want to know where the clearance rack is, and then someone moved it again because the front tables have to look “seasonal,” which is funny because seasonal used to mean actual seasons and now it’s like Valentine’s candy in January—\n\nSorry. What was the… feeling flat? I don’t think I’d call it that. I’m maybe quieter when I get home, but sometimes that’s just being tired. I’m not sure I’ve noticed a big change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed trouble remembering things that happened recently—like forgetting a conversation you had an hour ago, or forgetting where you put something you just used?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like that, no. I mean, I misplace things sometimes—keys, the scanner at work, my coffee if I set it down near the break room sink—but everybody does that, don’t they?\n\nA conversation an hour ago… I might not remember every detail, especially if someone was talking while I was trying to finish something else. At work there’s always the register beeping or someone asking about returns, and the return policy changed, or maybe it didn’t change but the signs changed, which makes people think it changed—\n\nBut, no, I haven’t noticed forgetting whole conversations or anything like that. Not that I can say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have others noticed you doing things that seemed unusual or out of place for the situation—like odd behaviors at work, trouble keeping up with personal care or hygiene, or acting in ways that didn't fit what was going on around you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little. I don’t know if they’d say “unusual,” but at work I’ll realize I’ve been walking the same aisle for a while, like back and forth, fixing the same row of batteries. Those little packs are always crooked anyway, because people take one from the middle instead of the front, and then the hooks don’t line up—\n\nBut, yeah, sometimes I’m not really moving on to the next thing. Or I’ll be standing there holding the schedule clipboard, and the associates are kind of waiting for me to tell them where to go, and I’m just… looking at it. Then I have to sort of snap back and say, okay, registers or stockroom or whatever. So I guess that probably looks odd.\n\nDoctor: Compared to how you were doing at work and with relationships before these speech and thinking difficulties started, would you say your overall performance and functioning have noticeably declined since then?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has declined. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be the one they put on the busy weekend shifts, you know? Saturdays were always a mess—people with coupons that expired, returns without receipts, the whole line backed up—and I could usually keep it moving.\n\nNow they don’t really trust me to run those shifts anymore. My boss said after my performance review that if things don’t improve, I could be suspended. He said it kind of carefully, like he didn’t want to make it sound too harsh, but that’s what it was.\n\nAnd at home… my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle. Commitments with people, plans, that kind of thing. Sometimes people seem uncomfortable or confused around me, and then she smooths it over. So, yeah, compared to before, it’s worse.\n\nDoctor: Over these past six months, has this disorganized thinking and speech been present most of the time on an ongoing basis, or has it come and gone in separate episodes with periods where you felt completely back to normal?\n\nPatient: It’s been more… ongoing, I think. Not like I have a bad week and then I’m totally back to myself for a month or something.\n\nThe jumbled talking, or when I’m trying to explain something and it gets all tangled up—that’s been noticeable for about six months now. Same with the odd stops on the sales floor, where I’m just sort of stuck there and then realize people are waiting. Some days are better than others, sure, especially if the store’s quiet, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely normal in between.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your motivation to start or finish things you used to want to do—at home or with hobbies—even when you had the time and energy to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it motivation, exactly. I mean, I still think about doing things. Like at home I’ll see something that needs doing and I’ll think, okay, I should get to that, but then—well, sometimes it’s more that I get caught up in how to start it.\n\nHobbies… I don’t know. I used to tinker with little things around the house, but then you need the right screwdriver, and the little ones disappear somehow. I had a whole set in a drawer, and then one of them was in the junk bowl by the keys, which doesn’t make sense because that’s where the grocery cards are supposed to go.\n\nSo… maybe? I haven’t really noticed it as just not wanting to do things. It’s hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself 'stuck' staring at the clipboard or standing in the aisle, does your body actually feel frozen or unable to move, or are you just lost in your thoughts while your body could move if you tried?\n\nPatient: It’s not like my legs are locked, exactly. The batteries are right by the register endcap, and they’re always a mess because people grab the wrong size—AA, AAA, those little coin ones—and then I’m there longer than I should be. So, no, not frozen exactly. More stuck in the action or in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have people close to you—like your wife or coworkers—commented that your facial expressions or tone of voice seem less emotional or more flat than they used to be, even when you're talking about something that would normally make you happy or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anyone’s said it that way, like “flat.” My wife says I mumble sometimes, or that I sound like I’m not listening, but that’s not exactly the same thing, is it?\n\nAt work people mostly just want to know where they’re assigned, you know, register two or stocking or returns, and if I’m too quiet they’ll ask again. But I don’t remember anyone saying my face looks different. I mean, faces are funny anyway—some people smile at customers all day and then the minute they turn around it’s gone. Retail does that to you.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed comments about my expressions or my voice being less emotional. Not specifically."

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about medical conditions, neurological issues, or substance use that could explain the speech and thinking difficulties, but the provided symptom profile does not include any medical history, head injury, seizures, thyroid problems, alcohol use, or drug use item.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure. I mean, I lose track sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as forgetting.
